A careful self-audit caught and fixed the standing "no redundant own" and "no real/genuine/genuinely as filler" writing rules across every new entry (several first-draft titles and summaries had to be corrected, e.g. "the body's own overnight inflammation peak" to "the body's overnight inflammation peak"). `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint` clean across every touched/created file. `PURPLE_DIGEST_VERSION` bumped. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Also 2026-08-24: full parallel Vegan and Vegetarian tracks shipped for the 6-Week Meal Plan, phase 2 of 3 of the same larger request (chrononutrition reorg → diet tracks → research content).** Direct follow-up, after the chrononutrition phase below shipped: "Keep going straight into the vegan and vegetarian tracks now." Confirmed earlier as full, parallel 42-day tracks, not swap-suggestions or a smaller sampler.

**106 new curated recipes** (`scripts/add_vegan_meal_plan_breakfasts.py`, 42 breakfasts; `scripts/add_vegan_meal_plan_mains.py`, 64 lunch/dinner mains), every ingredient individually verified against the live database both before writing and after running (the resolution path turned out to key off `foods.base_name`, a separate column from `foods.name`, a real gap caught by a first verification pass that used the wrong column and returned dozens of false failures, corrected before anything shipped). Vegan breakfasts replace all 42 of the plan's own dairy/egg-based breakfasts (a real gap: this database has no plain, unsweetened, non-branded soy or coconut yogurt, so silken tofu blended into a real "tofu yogurt," soy milk paired with a nut butter, and a turmeric-and-nutritional-yeast tofu scramble do the same job Greek yogurt and egg do in the omnivore version). The 64 lunch/dinner mains are built by direct substitution, not from scratch: every real vegetable, aromatic, and seasoning from each meat/fish/feta/egg dish in the omnivore plan carries over unchanged (pulled from the live database's own ingredient rows, not re-guessed), only the animal protein is replaced with tofu, tempeh, seitan (vital wheat gluten), a legume, hearts of palm, or a "meaty" mushroom (king oyster mushroom standing in for shrimp/scallops, portobello for duck) matched to that dish's own cooking method and cuisine. Two existing recipes (`curated_side_teriyaki_baked_tofu`, `curated_salad_sesame_ginger_slaw`) had their only non-vegan ingredient, honey, swapped to maple syrup directly in place, a flavor-neutral fix that benefits every track rather than needing a duplicate vegan-only copy.

**Architecture needed no `lib/db.ts` changes at all**, confirmed before writing anything: `setUpMealPlan`/`addMealPlanDayToSchedule` already accept any `MealPlanDay[]` as a parameter rather than hardcoding `MEAL_PLAN` internally. New `lib/mealPlanVegan.ts`/`lib/mealPlanVegetarian.ts` (mirroring `lib/mealPlan.ts`'s own `ref()` helper, now exported for reuse) each walk the omnivore plan's own 42 days, reusing a dish's original id wherever it was already track-compatible and substituting the new vegan id only where actually needed. The vegetarian track needed far fewer changes than the vegan one: since the chrononutrition pass already made every omnivore breakfast dairy/egg-based, all 42 reuse that lineup unchanged, and the 7 feta/egg lunch/dinner dishes stay as their original dairy/egg version too (fine for vegetarian), only the 57 real meat/fish dishes get swapped, reusing the exact same vegan substitute built for that slot rather than a third, separate recipe. `app/(tabs)/schedule.tsx`'s `MealPlanLens` gained a `PopoverSelect` "Diet track" picker (Omnivore/Vegan/Vegetarian) driving which array feeds the existing "Set Up My 6-Week Plan" button and the per-day manual-add list, no other UI restructuring needed since both paths already took a `mealPlan` parameter.

Every one of the 132 recipe-id references in each new file individually cross-verified against the live database in both directions (every id used resolves to a real row, every new row is actually used), and checked for accidental repeats within each track, matching this plan's own standing "no meal repeats within the 6 weeks" rule. `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint` clean across every touched/created file (a handful of pre-existing, unrelated lint issues elsewhere in `schedule.tsx` confirmed via `git diff` and left untouched). `REFERENCE_DB_VERSION` bumped, forcing the same re-import on next launch every prior reference-database patch already does. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Also 2026-08-24: the 6-Week Meal Plan's own breakfasts and dinner starch timing reorganized for chrononutrition, phase 1 of 3 of a larger request.** Direct follow-up: "it seems like you used a smoothie for breakfast more often than not... this whole app revolves around working synergistically with the way your body actually works... Please go through all recipes for each day, reorganizing foods to match the requirements of the needs of the body throughout the day." A shared Google AI Mode conversation came with the request; independently fact-checked against real research (chrononutrition is a genuine, active field with real cohort studies and RCTs) rather than taken at face value -- one of its own central claims (that eating late blocks autophagy) turned out to be more settled in animal studies than in humans, a 2024 human study found no measurable difference in autophagy markers between fasted and fed states, reported honestly rather than repeated as fact. The scale of the full request (chrononutrition reorg + full vegan/vegetarian tracks + Basic Health and condition research) was confirmed directly before starting: reorg first, diet tracks and research content as tracked follow-ups.

**The real gap an audit found, wider than the direct report itself named:** not just the 6 Smoothies (all clustered at the very start of the plan, days 1-6, which is what made it feel more frequent than its true 6-of-42 rate), but 13 more breakfasts -- every overnight-oats, warm-porridge, and chia-pudding recipe -- used almond or coconut milk as their own liquid base, carrying almost no protein and no fermented element either. All 19 fixed the same way: swapped to a real Greek yogurt base (new `scripts/chrononutrition_breakfast_protein_boost.py`, patching both the live database and matching `lib/digest/recipes.ts` entries), adding real protein and making the breakfast itself a fermented food. Three "coconut"-named recipes keep half their coconut milk rather than a full swap, so the flavor the name promises stays real; the two warm porridges keep a reduced amount of almond milk for the actual cooking liquid and get the yogurt stirred in as a real finishing step instead, since yogurt heated directly in a simmering pot tends to split. Every added honey amount was made optional rather than removed outright -- a small amount of real honey next to a large amount of whole fruit isn't the processed "hidden sugar" the research is actually warning against, but making it optional still honors the direct instruction that sugar doesn't belong in breakfast by default. Every one of the 42 breakfasts now has a real protein anchor (egg, yogurt, or cottage cheese); none did before.

**Separately, 6 days (9, 10, 17, 34, 36, 42) had their lunch and dinner swapped**, no new recipes needed: each one's dinner slot held a dedicated grain (brown rice, wild rice, or couscous) while its own lunch was already a lighter, grain-free salad or soup. Real evidence points the other way -- insulin sensitivity is highest earlier in the day, and a lighter, lower-starch dinner eaten well before bed is associated with better cardiometabolic outcomes in a real 103,000-person cohort (NutriNet-Santé, Nature Communications 2023). Whole-food starchy vegetables at dinner (potato, sweet potato, parsnip, turnip) were deliberately left alone rather than swapped, treated as a real, fiber-rich food, not the same concern as a dedicated refined-grain side.

`REFERENCE_DB_VERSION` and `APP_VERSION` (1.0.25.11) both bumped, `tsc`/`eslint` clean project-wide, every recipe id in `lib/mealPlan.ts` re-verified to still resolve after the swaps. **Not yet confirmed on-device.** Still open, tracked directly rather than silently dropped: full parallel vegan and vegetarian 42-day tracks (confirmed as the intended scope, the largest remaining piece), and new chrononutrition research content across Basic Health and as many of the 19 conditions as a real connection exists for (a first research pass already found a strong one for autoimmune thyroid disease specifically -- circadian clock disruption in autoimmune thyroiditis, European Thyroid Journal 2023 -- and a real chronotherapy trial for rheumatoid arthritis, the CAPRA-1 trial).

Weeks 3-6 (days 15-42) had none: every eligible Smoothie, Salad, Soup, Handheld, and Side had already been used exactly once by day 14. This batch is **84 brand-new recipes**, written and verified the same way the first 22 were, deliberately reaching for whole foods the first two weeks never touched: halibut, trout, tuna, sardine, mackerel, scallop, mussel, crab, lamb, duck, bison, pork chop, pork loin, turkey thigh, kiwi, papaya, peach, apricot, plum, nectarine, clementine, grapefruit, fig, date, watermelon, cantaloupe, fennel, parsnip, leek, radish, turnip, collards, kohlrabi, bok choy, okra, artichoke, snow peas, edamame, kidney beans, pinto beans, lima beans, buckwheat, millet, spelt (a real, honestly-disclosed stand-in for farro, since this database has no farro row of its own), bulgur, wild rice, couscous, pistachio, and cashew, alongside deliberate, honest repeats of a few realistic everyday staples (chicken, turkey, salmon, shrimp) in genuinely different dishes each time, the same way an actual person's own 6 weeks of home cooking would work. No two of the 42 days share the same breakfast/lunch/dinner combination.

New `scripts/add_meal_plan_recipes_weeks3to6.py` (same pattern as the first batch's `scripts/add_meal_plan_recipes.py`) seeded 84 `curated_recipes` rows and 489 `curated_recipe_ingredients` rows, every single ingredient individually verified against the live database before being written in, confirmed afterward with a direct query that all 489 rows resolve to something real (a CRLF artifact from the `sqlite3` CLI's own output briefly made that check look like it failed, caught and corrected before trusting the false result). All 84 new recipes were added to `lib/digest/recipes.ts` as full `DigestEntry`s, matching the same `RecipeCard` shape and writing discipline as the first batch. `lib/mealPlan.ts`'s own 42-day rotation is now complete, cross-verified a second way: every single `curatedRecipeId` it references was confirmed to exist both in `recipes.ts` and as a real row in the live database, not just assumed from having written both by hand.

**Most recent (2026-08-24): the first 2 weeks of a real 6-Week Whole-Food Meal Plan, with a working auto-scheduler and a new, general shopping-list feature.** Direct request: "at least 6 weeks worth of meals that can be automatically loaded so the user can then receive a full shopping list for all ingredients to be purchased fresh every 3 to 4 days. A different meal set every day without repeating meals within that 6 weeks or more and providing the user an array of all possible fruits and vegetables to provide the largest different number of nutrients possible and still gives them as close to the RDA for all nutrients." A shared Google AI Mode conversation link came with the request, read via the same Chrome-tab-plus-`get_page_text` workaround as prior batches; it turned out to be generic advice confirming the exact copyright discipline this Digest already follows for its other 88 recipes (ingredient lists are facts nobody owns, only someone's specific instructional wording is protected), so nothing new to adopt from it, just confirmation the existing approach is sound. Three scoping questions asked and answered directly before building: full breakfast/lunch/dinner every day (not just one meal), both an auto-schedule button AND an individual/manual add-to-schedule path, and this stays behind the same access level as the rest of full Recipes/Schedule (checked directly: no real tier-gating enforcement exists anywhere in the shipped app yet, so nothing new to gate today).

**Honest scale, named directly rather than glossed over:** 42 days x 3 meals is 126 unique meals. This batch ships a real, complete, working first 2 weeks (14 days, 42 meals), reusing 20 of the existing 88 recipes as-is (all 6 Smoothies for breakfast, all 6 Salads, all 4 Soups, and all 4 Handhelds for lunch/dinner, each used exactly once) plus 22 brand-new recipes written from scratch (7 breakfasts, 4 lunches, 11 dinners), deliberately reaching for produce not already used elsewhere in the plan (salmon, pork tenderloin, cod, shrimp, beef, tofu, chicken thigh, turkey breast and ground turkey, brussels sprouts, asparagus, zucchini, eggplant, green lentils, white beans, mushroom, cottage cheese, and more). The remaining 28 days (weeks 3-6) are a real, tracked continuation, not forgotten, the same "ongoing, multi-session" shape this Digest's own biggest content batches already follow: extending it is purely additive (more recipes.ts entries, more lib/mealPlan.ts days), no architecture changes needed.

**Architecture reuses almost everything that already existed**, confirmed by reading the real code rather than assumed: `getCuratedRecipe()` (already how "Build This Recipe" resolves a curated recipe) feeds straight into one of the 11 already-existing `saveX()` builder-save functions (a new small dispatcher, `saveComponentFromCuratedPayload` in `lib/db.ts`, is the only missing piece there), and `scheduleMeal()`/`saveMealFavorite()` (both already existed, built 2026-08-15) place the result on a specific date. New `lib/mealPlan.ts` holds only the day-by-day REFERENCES (which recipe fills which slot), deliberately kept separate from `lib/digest/recipes.ts`'s own recipe CONTENT. New `lib/db.ts` functions: `setUpMealPlan(startDate, plan)` (the bulk button, re-run-safe, skips a day/slot already scheduled rather than double-booking), `addMealPlanDayToSchedule(day, date)` (the manual per-day path), and `getUpcomingShoppingList(daysAhead)`, a genuinely new capability confirmed not to exist anywhere before this (no code resolved a still-*planned*, not-yet-eaten scheduled meal's real ingredients) built from real existing pieces (`getMealFavorite()` -> its `components[]` -> the already-existing `getComponentIngredients()` dispatcher, aggregated by category/name/unit). Works for any planned meal in range, not just plan-generated ones, so it's a real, general Schedule capability. A real dinner can pair a main dish with a distinct side (`MealPlanSlot.side`, optional), so the same small pool of real side dishes can appear across several different day-to-day pairings without that counting as a repeated meal, the same way an actual person's home cooking rotates sides.

**New UI:** two new Schedule lenses (`app/(tabs)/schedule.tsx`), matching the same LensHub pattern every other lens already uses. "Meal Plan": a start-date field plus "Set Up My 6-Week Plan" button for the bulk path, and the full day list below with its own per-day date field and "Add to Schedule" button for the manual path. "Shopping List": a 3/4/7-day window picker, ingredients grouped by the same category taxonomy `FoodLookup` already shelves by.

**A real, named architectural call, not silently made:** no "meal"/"entree" builder type exists among this app's real 11 direct-ingredient builders, so every new full protein dinner main (salmon, pork, chicken, beef, tofu) uses `'side'`, this app's own genuinely generic single-dish builder (its own Food-tab copy already describes it as "any name/ingredients," never restricted to small vegetable portions), the closest real fit among what actually exists rather than inventing a new builder type. The two stews (green lentil, turkey black bean chili) use `'soup'`, a better real fit. Full reasoning in `scripts/add_meal_plan_recipes.py`'s own header comment.

Every new ingredient individually verified against the live reference database before being written in (same discipline as every prior curated-recipe batch), confirmed with a direct post-run query that all 162 new ingredient rows resolve to something real. `REFERENCE_DB_VERSION` and `APP_VERSION` (now 1.0.25.9) both bumped, `tsc`/`eslint` clean project-wide. **Not yet confirmed on-device** -- this is the first genuinely new, multi-part feature shipped this way in a while, worth a real look before trusting it fully: the bulk button, the manual per-day add, and the shopping list all touch real persistence that only this code path exercises so far.

**Most recent (2026-08-24): every curated recipe in the Digest, and the live database that actually builds them, rescaled from 2 people down to 1.** Direct request: "For all recipes throughout the Digest, reduce the size for each recipe to be only for one person. This app will begin for a person not knowing that they have more than one person living in their home with them. The app can do the math to increase the ingredients to accommodate for additional people. Make absolute sure that the recipe lines up appropriately for every ingredient to align with the requirements of just one person." A household-size setting isn't collected anywhere yet, so 1 is the only honest default, and scaling a known recipe up for more people later is far simpler than guessing a stranger's household down from an assumed 2.

Both halves of every one of the 88 curated recipes were touched by hand, category by category, not run through a blind halving script: `lib/digest/recipes.ts`'s own `yield` text and `recipeCard.ingredients` (whole units like eggs, tortillas, and garlic cloves rounded to a real, buyable amount rather than left as an odd fraction), and the live `curated_recipes`/`curated_recipe_ingredients` tables in `assets/data/foods_reference.db`, the structured data that actually populates a Food builder when someone taps "Build This Recipe." Three distinct prior states had to be reconciled per category rather than assuming one uniform rule: Sides, Soups, and Desserts had their own database rows still stuck at an original 4-serving design from an earlier, never-fully-applied 4-to-2 rescale, so those got quartered rather than halved; Smoothies, Electrolyte Water, Golden Milk, both single-serving Snacks, the Turkey Wrap, and the Grilled Chicken Sandwich already had their database rows sitting at a correct single-serving value from before an earlier doubling pass, so those needed no database change at all, just a text revert; everything else (Baked Goods, most Beverages, Fermentation basics, Sauces, Salads, most Handhelds, both single-dose ferment concentrates, and all 29 Fermented Drinks) had recipes.ts and the database already agreeing on a 2-person design, so both were halved together. `nutritionHighlights` was left untouched everywhere its own stated serving size didn't change, since shrinking a recipe's total yield down to 1 serving doesn't change what's already true about that one serving's own nutrition.

The database side ran through a new `scripts/rescale_curated_recipes_to_1_person.py`, applying the exact multiplier decided for each recipe (0.5, 0.25, or no change) to every one of that recipe's own ingredient rows and to `curated_recipes.servings`, leaving `serving_size_amount`/`serving_size_unit` fixed since the size of one serving never changed, only how many the recipe now yields. Spot-checked directly against the database after running (Herb-Roasted Root Vegetables, the whole wheat bread loaf, the egg salad wraps' 2 eggs, the trail mix, the tart cherry tonic, the baked cinnamon apples) and every value matched the new recipes.ts text exactly. `REFERENCE_DB_VERSION` bumped, forcing the same re-import on next launch every prior reference-database patch already does. `tsc` clean project-wide, `eslint` clean on every touched file. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Most recent (2026-08-24): a corpus-wide sweep removing the redundant "own" filler tic from every Digest content file, roughly 2,000 instances.** Direct correction, quoting a specific example: "Unlike glyphosate's own still-disputed cancer classification... Why does so much of the data in the app still have this ownership thing going on? This is not how people talk... The word 'own' has no place there at all. Please go through this entire app's writings and make sure the word is appropriately used and not AI used." Correct: "X's own Y" (the study's own conclusion, the trial's own authors, this category's own research) had become a pervasive, mechanical tic, distinct from the earlier "real/genuine/genuinely" filler-word rule and not yet caught by it.

Scale check done first: 2,854 raw "'s own"/"its own"/"their own" matches across `lib/digest/*.ts` alone. A blind find-replace was ruled out directly, since some uses carry actual meaning (the "in its own right"/"on its own terms" idiom family, the standalone "on X's own" meaning "independently," and this app's own legitimate autoimmune-disease framing, "the immune system attacks the body's own tissue," where "own" carries meaningful self-versus-foreign medical distinction). A scripted, staged pass instead: a Node script skipping code comments entirely (only customer-facing `title`/`teaser`/`summary`/`stageNote` text touched) and skipping any removal where the possessor was animate/personal (body, person, someone, patient, mother, wife, gut, immune, thyroid) or the following word was body/tissue-related, run in dry-run first, spot-checked, then applied.

Two distinct bugs caught only by careful post-apply auditing, not assumed clean from a passing type-check alone: first, the script's regex required a literal apostrophe, silently missing every instance inside a single-quoted string using an escaped apostrophe (`glyphosate\'s own`), a second pass with a corrected regex caught 366 more, including the exact glyphosate example quoted in the request. Second, stripping "own" from the "on its own [verb]" idiom (meaning "independently") left broken grammar in a small number of cases ("on its own does not" losing "own" and "does not" both) since the word right after "own" was a verb, not a noun; a systematic, three-tier verification (a narrow verb/conjunction stopword list checked against re-derived original text, a broader idiom-noun restoration for "right"/"terms"/"merits", and a "deserves/worth its own coverage" template restoration run twice after a second pass re-broke it once) found and fixed all instances, confirmed by a final zero-result sweep. `tsc` clean project-wide, `eslint` clean across all 58 touched files, zero "own own" duplicates, the exact quoted example confirmed fixed. Standing writing-style rule updated to cover this going forward. **Not yet confirmed on-device** (a pure text change, low visual risk, but not yet read through by a human on the actual app).

**Also 2026-08-24: a deep dive on the same organic-farming topic, direct follow-up, 10 more Earth Matters/Home Gardening entries.** Direct pushback on the batch just below: "I believe there are far more than just 4 new entries that could be gotten from this. Please do a deep dive into the topic and look for more that can be added to the Earth Matters and Gardening areas." Correct: the first pass covered the yield-gap and India case studies the source conversation named explicitly, but left the underlying soil-biology mechanism it opened with, and several adjacent, well-evidenced topics, unwritten.

Checked against every existing title in both categories first, not guessed at, to find actual gaps rather than duplicate anything already covered (compost and cover crops both already had dedicated entries; a condition-specific check was skipped this round since it was already established that this topic doesn't connect to any of the 19 tracked conditions).

7 new Earth Matters entries in `lib/digest/foodIndustryHistory.ts`: the soil food web's actual mechanism (nitrogen mineralization, the trophic-grazing process the source conversation's own "billions of bacteria, fungi, and worms" line was gesturing at without explaining); the rhizobia-legume nitrogen-fixation symbiosis (the actual mechanism behind "cover crops add nitrogen," root nodules, nitrogenase, symbiosomes); what "certified organic" actually legally requires under USDA's National Organic Program (the 3-year land transition, the allowed/prohibited substance rule, documented inspection and testing enforcement); biodynamic farming, an honest correction (organic methods underneath, unproven ritual "preparations" on top, backed by a peer-reviewed literature review finding little evidence for the preparations specifically); farmland ownership concentration, the documentary's own "Who Owns the World?" question taken seriously (foreign ownership at a measured 3.61% of US farmland, with the same researchers naming domestic consolidation into fewer, larger operations as the bigger structural story); a UC Davis 19-year field trial (the Century Experiment) finding compost, not cover crops alone, is what actually locks carbon deep in soil, a direct complication to a claim this category's own prior content treated as settled; and Korean Natural Farming/JADAM, a broader Asian counterpart to the already-covered India ZBNF case study, tiered moderate since its own adoption data is farmer-reported, not yet a controlled trial.

3 new Home Gardening entries in `lib/digest/homeGardening.ts`: what "organic" pesticides a home gardener can actually buy and use (Bt, spinosad, copper, correcting the assumption that organic means pesticide-free); hot/thermophilic composting (the specific temperature-and-time thresholds that actually kill weed seeds and pathogens, distinct from the existing basic-composting entry); and the Three Sisters companion-planting system (a USDA-confirmed yield efficiency figure, Land Equivalent Ratio 1.2-1.4, plus an honest complication that the nitrogen benefit mostly helps next year's crop, not this year's). Both closing synthesis cards got their relatedIds extended to cover the new content. `tsc` clean project-wide, `eslint` clean on both touched files, every new `relatedIds` entry confirmed to resolve. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Also 2026-08-24: a third shared Google AI Mode conversation, this one on the science of organic farming, fact-checked and turned into 5 new Earth Matters/Home Gardening entries.** Same request pattern as the two research batches above ("move on to this one and do the same thing"), a third `share.google/aimode/...` link, read via the same Chrome-tab-plus-`get_page_text` workaround. This conversation's own topic (soil biology, natural fertility inputs, and organic-versus-conventional crop yields, referencing the documentary "Who Owns the World? - Food") isn't about any of the 19 tracked human conditions at all, so unlike the two prior batches this one has no condition-specific angle to check for, checked and correctly excluded as a category rather than force-fit onto anything. It maps instead to this Digest's own existing Earth Matters (agricultural science and policy) and Home Gardening (practical growing guidance) categories.

An overlap check against both categories' own existing content came first: Basic composting and cover cropping already had their own dedicated Home Gardening entries, so this batch covers what wasn't there yet. One figure from the source conversation is deliberately dropped rather than repeated: its claim that India's own overall organic yield gap runs "around 5%" couldn't be traced to any specific, checkable study, replaced with the actual, checkable range that does exist (a Kenya smallholder study and an India-specific cotton field trial, both showing the gap is measurable but crop-dependent, not one flat number).

4 new Earth Matters entries in `lib/digest/foodIndustryHistory.ts`: the global organic-versus-conventional yield gap (Seufert et al. 2012, Nature, the landmark meta-analysis, 25% lower overall dropping to 13% with best organic management, and close to no gap at all for legumes and perennials); the context-dependent version of that same gap (narrows or reverses under drought, in low-input smallholder systems, and as soil recovers over a variable, not fixed, transition period); India's Zero Budget Natural Farming as its own case study (a controlled, multi-season, multi-district field trial finding ZBNF groundnut yields 30-40% higher than conventional, not just organic); and Sikkim's 2016 transition to the world's first fully organic state (the actual FAO policy-award recognition, reported alongside its own documented economic tradeoffs, market access, certification cost, food-security concerns, an honest mixed picture rather than a clean win).

1 new Home Gardening entry in `lib/digest/homeGardening.ts`: the natural fertility amendments not yet covered (aged manure's own documented burn-risk safety rule, bone meal versus blood meal's distinctly different nutrient profiles, and kelp/seaweed extract's peer-reviewed cytokinin/biostimulant effect, distinct from a plain mineral-content claim). Both closing "tying together" synthesis cards got a light-touch relatedIds addition pointing at the new content rather than a full rewrite. `tsc` clean project-wide, `eslint` clean on both touched files, every new `relatedIds` entry confirmed to resolve. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

There should not yet be specific stories or parts of them until I select one of the subsection header links... I want it throughout the entire Digest sections. All of them."

**Also 2026-08-24: a second shared Google AI Mode conversation, this one on diet's effect on ADHD and OCD, fact-checked and turned into 6 new Digest entries.** Same direct request pattern as the mental-health batch just above, applied to a second conversation. Read via the same Chrome browser workaround (WebFetch still can't reach the actual content of a `share.google/aimode/...` link, a navigated tab plus `get_page_text` can). Neither ADHD nor OCD is one of this app's 19 tracked conditions, so this content lives entirely in Basic Health except for one condition-specific finding that turned up during the 19-condition check.

A discrepancy caught while fact-checking, not just a citation gap: the source conversation's own first answer claimed 78% of children improved on an elimination diet with a 63% relapse rate, then its own later answer, citing the same underlying Lancet (INCA) trial, gave a different figure, 64% improved. The actual published trial result is 64% (32 of 50 children), confirmed directly against the primary source. The 78%/63% figures are dropped rather than carried into this Digest.

5 new Basic Health entries in `lib/digest/mentalHealth.ts`: a framing entry stating plainly that diet doesn't cause either condition, both are neurobiological, while still measurably changing symptom severity; the two randomized Lancet trials behind ADHD's own dietary-trigger evidence (the INCA elimination-diet trial, corrected to its actual 64% figure, and the Southampton artificial-food-color/sodium-benzoate trial); omega-3, zinc, and glycemic-stability evidence for ADHD, cross-linked to the existing EPA/DHA and glycemic-instability entries rather than repeating their own mechanisms; OCD's own distinct gut-brain research (reduced short-chain-fatty-acid capacity and inflammatory-pathway enrichment correlating with OCD severity specifically, cross-linked to the existing SCFA-mood mechanism entry rather than duplicated); and the ketogenic diet's early evidence for OCD (a 3-patient case series averaging a 90.5% symptom-score reduction, honestly tiered weak since case series and case reports can't rule out placebo or regression to the mean).

1 new condition-specific entry: Celiac Disease (`celiac.ts`), ADHD-like symptoms tracking with gluten-free diet compliance, reported honestly as a mixed evidence base (a review of the same literature found no conclusive proof of a direct ADHD-celiac link), distinct from celiac's own existing depression/anxiety entry. The other 18 conditions were checked and correctly left without a new entry, this conversation's own claims run through ADHD/OCD-specific neurobiology (dopamine/norepinephrine pathways, CSTC-circuit glutamate signaling) that this Digest's own research doesn't tie to any of them specifically. `tsc` clean project-wide, `eslint` clean on both touched files, every new `relatedIds` entry confirmed to resolve. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Also 2026-08-24: a shared Google AI Mode conversation on diet and mental illness, fact-checked and turned into 7 new Digest entries.** Direct request: read the linked conversation, research the factualness of everything, expand to how it affects the 19 tracked conditions, and add entries to Basic Health and the relevant conditions. The conversation itself wasn't directly readable via WebFetch (both the raw share link and its own server-supplied redirect returned a generic Google Search error page rather than the actual content); a Chrome browser tab navigated to the same URL rendered the full conversation, which a plain `get_page_text` call then captured in full.

Every claim was independently re-verified via WebSearch rather than trusted as given, matching this app's own standing discipline, and two of the source conversation's own claims needed a direct correction rather than a citation: its "toxicity" framing turned out to mean diet-driven inflammation, not literal poisoning, and its implied "gut serotonin reaches the brain and shapes mood directly" framing is wrong, peripheral serotonin (including the kind gut cells make) does not cross the blood-brain barrier, the documented mechanism runs through short-chain fatty acids instead. An overlap check ran before writing anything: this Digest already had a dedicated Mental Health & Food topic (inflammation-CRP, the SMILES trial, EPA/DHA, exercise, B12/folate) and an individually-cited depression/anxiety comorbidity entry for all 19 conditions, so most of the source conversation's own claims were already covered. What wasn't: ultra-processed food intake itself as a diet-quality exposure (distinct from two conditions merely co-occurring), the actual gut-to-mood mechanism, blood sugar instability as a short-term mood driver, and depression-specific evidence for vitamin D, magnesium, and zinc.

5 new Basic Health entries in `lib/digest/mentalHealth.ts`: ultra-processed food intake and depression/anxiety risk (a 2022 meta-analysis of 385,541 people, 44% higher odds of depressive symptoms and 48% higher odds of anxiety symptoms at the highest intake level, honestly flagged as observational, not causal, and cross-checked against a second meta-analysis that found the depression link but not the anxiety one, a disagreement reported rather than smoothed over); the actual gut-microbiome-to-mood mechanism (fiber fermented into short-chain fatty acids, not serotonin transport, with the blood-brain-barrier correction named directly and cross-linked to IBS's own existing, and separately accurate, gut-serotonin-motility entry); blood sugar instability and insulin resistance as a shared mood mechanism; vitamin D and depression (honestly tiered as a mixed, evolving evidence base, explicitly not overriding this Digest's own existing conservative supplementation guidance); and magnesium/zinc deficiency and mood.

2 new condition-specific entries, added only where the insulin-resistance-mood mechanism was genuinely new rather than already covered by that condition's own existing bidirectional-risk entry: Type 2 Diabetes (`type2Diabetes.ts`) and PCOS (`pcos.ts`), both cross-linked to each other and to the new Basic Health mechanism entry. The other 16 non-excluded conditions were checked and correctly left without a new entry, since their own existing depression/anxiety comorbidity entries (built in an earlier session) already cover this ground and a second, redundant entry would only duplicate it. Prostate Health was checked and excluded: its own documented mental health connection is diagnosis-related distress (fear of recurrence, body image), not a diet-driven mechanism, so it didn't fit this specific research task. `tsc` clean project-wide, `eslint` clean on all 4 touched files, every new `relatedIds` entry individually confirmed to resolve to an existing id before shipping. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Also 2026-08-24: the collapsed sprout badge, nudged a little farther into the corner.** "Can we or can we not move the shrunken sprout farther left and a little bit higher? Left by about 5 to 10 pixels and up by about 3 to 5." Yes, a small position adjustment: `greetingCollapsedWrap` moved from `top: 4, left: 4` to `top: 0, left: -3`, landing near the middle of each given range (7px left, 4px up). `tsc`/`eslint` clean, one file touched. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Most recent (2026-08-23): the expanded greeting card's background, second round.** "That's a little better but should be even less transparent." Dropping the extra `opacity: 0.92` layer helped, but `colors.surface` itself is only ~85% opaque by design, fine for a card sitting in normal page flow, not solid enough for one floating directly on top of everything else. Switched to `colors.menuSurface`, a fully opaque color (no alpha channel at all) already established in this app for exactly this job, TabHub's own popup menu was deliberately kept opaque the same way, for the same reason. Not theme-reactive the way `colors.surface` is (one fixed color regardless of the person's own chosen ground theme), an accepted tradeoff here since staying solid above everything else matters more right now than matching the ground theme precisely. `tsc`/`eslint` clean, one file touched. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Most recent (2026-08-23): direct report, the expanded greeting card's own background.** "When tapped, the welcome screen needs to have a more solid background because right now I can see right through it." The `opacity: 0.92` this card carried on top of `colors.surface`'s own already-baked-in ~85% opacity (see that token's own comment in `constants/colors.ts`) compounded into something visibly see-through, not the subtle floating-overlay effect the earlier "a little more transparent" request was actually asking for. Removed outright rather than tuned to a smaller number: this card now reads exactly as solid as every other `colors.surface` card on this screen, since staying readable when tapped open matters more than a faded look, and its own absolute positioning already reads as "floating over the content" without needing an extra transparency layer on top of that. `tsc`/`eslint` clean, one file touched. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Most recent (2026-08-23): four more direct follow-ups on the greeting card, one message.** "Let's take it down to 30 seconds before it shrinks. Make it so while it is visible, the sprout is full color and only goes to 50% transparency after. And, it should go farther into the corner, sort of in the margin. It needs to be visible but not take away from the other things as much as possible. And, if the user swipes to either side or chooses another tab, the welcome shrinks right then and stays shrunk until they select it again."

(1) The initial full-size display's own auto-collapse timer dropped from 60000ms to 30000ms, now matching the 'expanded' state's own already-30-second auto-close, kept as its own separate effect regardless since they're conceptually different triggers that happen to currently share one number. (2) `renderGreetingSeedBadge`'s existing `action: 'collapse' | 'expand'` parameter already drew exactly the right line: `'collapse'` only ever happens while the card is currently full-size and visible, `'expand'` only ever happens once it's already shrunk to just the badge. A new `dimmed = action === 'expand'` derives the icon's own look from that same parameter rather than threading a second prop that would only ever move in lockstep with it, applying a new `greetingSeedIconDimmed` (`opacity: 0.5`) only in the standalone-collapsed case; the corner badge inside a full-size card (either `'initial'` or `'expanded'`) now stays full color. (3) `greetingCollapsedWrap` moved from `top: 12, left: 20` (matching the full card's own padding) to `top: 4, left: 4`, sitting closer to the true screen edge once shrunk, a deliberately different, smaller offset than the full card's own rather than the same constant reused. (4) A new, dedicated `useFocusEffect` whose only job is its own cleanup function: React Navigation fires that cleanup on blur (swiping away or picking another tab), exactly the moment named in the request, setting `greetingCardState` to `'collapsed'` unconditionally (a harmless no-op re-set if it already was). Home staying mounted across tab switches, already relied on elsewhere on this same screen, is what makes "stays shrunk until they select it again" true without any extra bookkeeping: there's no remount here to reset it back.

`tsc`/`eslint` clean, one file touched. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Most recent (2026-08-23): direct follow-up, the seed badge's own square.** "Can we make it so the sprout has a transparent background completely and no border around the square? And then make the sprout about 50% transparent, too." `greetingSeedBadge` (the same shared square wrapping the seed icon in all three places it appears, see that render function's own comment) lost its `backgroundColor`/`borderWidth`/`borderColor` entirely, leaving only its size and centering, purely a tap target now. `greetingSeedIcon` gained `opacity: 0.5`. The opacity sits on the icon specifically, not the badge that wraps it, the same interactive-element-versus-what's-drawn split this screen already uses elsewhere (`FlipCard`'s own `borderColor` prop, the expanded card's own `opacity`), so the tappable hit area itself stays full-size and unfaded even though what's visible inside it is now half-transparent. `tsc`/`eslint` clean, one file touched. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Most recent (2026-08-23): the greeting card collapses into a floating seed badge, and every other Home section becomes reorderable.** Direct request: "The top box on Home screen that welcomes them takes up a lot of space, but it is the only one really that is specific to the home screen and should always be available. Can it be shrunk into a small square floating on top of everything else, and have it display for 1 minute after first opening the app, then shrink to the square with some specific symbol on it... Actually, maybe this is where we make sure the sprouting seed default TabHub button continues to be used. Make it have that as a small version of it on the top left corner of the card, and then it shrinks into that sprouting seed on a little square that stays at the top left of the available screen. If tapped on it expands to the regular size of it so they can view the data there. After 30 seconds it closes again. It appears over the top of anything else on the home screen but is a little more transparent so it isn't distracting. When it first shrinks after opening the app, all of the other entities can then move up. Also, they should be able to move the things on the home screen they have chosen to be there into any order they want to from top to bottom, except the welcome box."

**The greeting card.** New `greetingCardState`, a plain `'initial' | 'collapsed' | 'expanded'` piece of session state, not a saved preference. `'initial'`: full size, in normal document flow, unchanged from before, for 60 seconds after Home first mounts (a plain `useEffect` timer, not `useFocusEffect`, so it fires once per app open, not every tab switch back to Home, since Home stays mounted across tab switches). Then `'collapsed'`: the card leaves document flow entirely (everything below it moves up to fill the space, exactly as asked) and a small floating seed badge, pinned at the top left of `contentArea` (already positioned below `ScreenHeader`, so no separate safe-area math was needed), takes its place as a sibling of the ScrollView, the same "floats over the scrollable content" technique this screen's own `bottomMask`/`EdgeShadow` already use. Tapping it goes to `'expanded'`: the full card again, at the same top-left origin so it visibly grows back out of the badge, floating over the already-moved-up content rather than back in document flow, at a modest `opacity: 0.92` (background and text together, deliberately mild since every text style already carries its own `textShadow`) so it reads as temporary. A second `useEffect`, keyed on `greetingCardState` itself, auto-collapses it again after 30 seconds, cleared early if the badge itself is tapped first.

The badge is the same `seed-tall-transparent.png` the TabHub button's own default icon already uses, not new art, appearing in three places with one shared render function: the corner of the full card in both `'initial'` and `'expanded'` (tap to collapse), and standing alone as the whole of `'collapsed'` (tap to expand). A bug caught before shipping: the first version hardcoded the badge's own tap to always collapse, which would have made the standalone collapsed badge do nothing when tapped (already collapsed). Fixed with an `action: 'collapse' | 'expand'` parameter instead of one fixed behavior.

**Reordering.** New `homeSectionOrder: HomeSectionKey[]` on `VisualPreferences`, following the same "absence means default" contract `homeSectionVisibility` already established, reconciled through a new `getOrderedHomeSectionKeys()` rather than read directly, so a saved order missing a section added to Home later still shows that section (appended in its own designed position) instead of silently hiding it. New `REORDERABLE_HOME_SECTION_KEYS`, `ALL_HOME_SECTION_KEYS` minus `'weather'`, derived rather than hand-duplicated: the welcome box itself was never a `HomeSectionKey` to begin with, and `weather` specifically renders embedded inside that same fixed box, not as an independent section, so excluding it from reordering was already implied by the existing architecture, not a new exception invented for this request.

Profile's own "Home Screen" card gained a new "Order" list below the existing visibility toggles: one row per reorderable section, up and down buttons rather than drag-and-drop (no drag library exists in this project yet, and a plain tap matches the low-risk-control convention every other picker on this screen already follows). Home's own render logic was restructured to match: the 9 reorderable sections, each already a self-contained block of JSX, were pulled into their own functions (`renderSymptomCheckinReminder`, `renderTodaysCheckin`, etc., every one unchanged from its own prior JSX, just relocated) and dispatched through one `renderHomeSection(key)` switch, called in whatever order `getOrderedHomeSectionKeys()` returns rather than a sequence hardcoded into the JSX itself. `digestCards` (the Digest flip cards), which used to render outside the loading gate the other 8 sections wait on, was folded into the same gate here: once any section can land anywhere in the order, one section skipping that gate wherever it happened to land would leave a real, confusing gap in the middle of the sequence while neighboring sections are still waiting, a deliberate, named simplification, not an oversight.

`tsc` clean project-wide; `eslint` clean on every touched file (one pre-existing unused-import caught and removed in the same pass, several more pre-existing unescaped-apostrophe warnings elsewhere in `profile.tsx`/`index.tsx` confirmed via `git diff` and left untouched). Four files touched (`app/(tabs)/index.tsx`, `app/profile.tsx`, `lib/visualPreferences.ts`, plus this document). **Not yet confirmed on-device**, and worth a direct on-device look given how much of this turns on real timing and layout behavior a type-checker can't verify on its own.

**Most recent (2026-08-23): direct follow-up, "Shouldn't the line color be the color for Digest?"** Correct: `FlipCard`'s own border used the plain, neutral `colors.border` while `moreFlipCard` sitting right next to it in the same row already used `colors.tabPurpleDigest`, an inconsistency the border-width fix earlier the same day fixed the thickness of but never touched the color of. `FlipCard` gained an optional `borderColor` prop (defaulting to the same neutral `colors.border` it always used, since this is a generic, reusable component, not Digest-specific on its own), the same "shared component, tab color passed in by the caller" shape `PopoverSelect`'s own `tabColor` prop already establishes elsewhere. Home's own call site passes `colors.tabPurpleDigest` explicitly. `tsc`/`eslint` clean, two files touched. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Most recent (2026-08-23): direct follow-up to Home's Digest flip cards, both a gap and a bug from the same-day rebuild.** "They don't link back to the information card in Digest where they refer to, and they cut off in mid sentence without baiting their appetite. If they can scroll within that little area, give enough to catch their interest, and end with a way to read more, which takes them to the specific card it comes from." Plus, separately: "I don't think the lines around the Digest knowledge boxes is as thick as the other entities on the home screen."

The link-back gap was confirmed: nothing before this let anything outside Digest deep-link straight to one specific entry, only to a whole category (`openDigestLens`). New `openEntryId` search param on `/purple-digest`, handled in the same `useFocusEffect` `openDigestLens` already uses, routed through `jumpToRelated` (the same function Related-entry chips already call), so a fresh arrival lands on the right category, the right topic, the entry itself expanded and scrolled into view, not a second, parallel navigation mechanism. The scroll-into-view race a fresh, not-yet-mounted category could hit is already handled by `scrollNodeIntoView`'s own existing 15-attempt `requestAnimationFrame` retry, proven by the same Related-chip use case, not something new built for this.

The mid-sentence cutoff was `FlipCard`'s own back face showing an entry's full summary (some run several hundred words) inside a fixed-height scrollable box, with nothing signaling there was more once it scrolled out of view. New `flipCardExcerpt()` (`app/(tabs)/index.tsx`) trims to a short excerpt, cut at the end of a sentence wherever one falls close enough to the limit rather than an arbitrary character count. `FlipCard` gained an `onReadMore` prop, rendered as a persistent "Read more →" link sitting outside the scrollable excerpt (always visible regardless of scroll position, a nested `TouchableOpacity` inside the card's own flip-toggle one, which React Native's touch responder system already resolves to the innermost handler without conflict), wired to the new `openEntryId` deep link.

The border-width report named a real gap in the same day's own earlier consistency pass: that pass audited `app/(tabs)/index.tsx` directly but never touched `components/FlipCard.tsx`, a separate file, still at the old 1px. Now matches `TAB_BORDER_WIDTH` (2px, the literal value only, not imported, so this shared component stays independent of one specific screen's own local constant).

`tsc` clean project-wide; `eslint` clean on every touched file (one new, deliberate `eslint-disable-next-line react-hooks/exhaustive-deps`, matching this same file's own existing precedent for the identical situation: a plain, non-memoized function referenced inside a `useFocusEffect` whose only real dependencies are stable setState setters and a ref, not anything that goes stale). Three files touched (`components/FlipCard.tsx`, `app/(tabs)/index.tsx`, `app/(tabs)/purple-digest.tsx`). **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Most recent (2026-08-23): Home's own flip cards now draw live from The Digest, condition-scoped, plus a font-shadow and border-width consistency pass.** Three direct reports in one message. (1) "I think these should only reflect things from the Digest that are either from the free tier info, or from their own conditions they have selected in their profile. However, from the profile they should be able to select to include data from any of the other conditions... this shouldn't mean that those conditions are now added to their own that the app tracks and helps with." (2) "all fonts on here will look better with a dropshadow on them... I see some here and there that don't have it." (3) "line thicknesses need to be consistent."

Investigating (1) surfaced a gap: Home's "A Few Things Worth Knowing" flip cards were never actually Digest-sourced despite the name, a fixed, hand-written array of 17 Hashimoto's/autoimmune tidbits, unrelated to which conditions someone actually has. Rebuilt as `digestFlipCardPool()`, a live filter over `ALL_DIGEST_ENTRIES` scoped to `basicHealth` (the free-tier content) plus the person's own selected conditions, memoized so filtering 1,500+ entries only recomputes when that scope actually changes. New `curious_about_conditions` table (`lib/db.ts`), deliberately separate from `user_conditions`, same shape, so nothing that reads the tracked-conditions table (scoring, medication rules, healing stages) ever sees a condition someone is merely curious about. New Profile sub-section, "Curious about other conditions," same `conditionGrid` pill layout as the existing picker, filtered to exclude whatever's already selected as one's own; selecting a condition as one's own also clears it from curious-about automatically, so the same condition never shows pinned in both lists. `CONDITION_CODE_TO_DIGEST_KEY` (already shared with Digest's own LensHub pinning) bridges the reference database's snake_case condition codes to this app's camelCase Digest categories. `ProblemFoodEntry`-shaped Digest entries (no `title`/`summary` of their own) are handled via the existing `isProblemFoodEntry` type guard rather than assumed away. Per-entry icons are gone (the old array hand-picked a different Ionicons glyph per tidbit); every card now uses `PurpleRibbonIcon`, tying each one visibly back to where it came from.

(2) and (3): audited `app/(tabs)/index.tsx` directly rather than guessed. Found 12 text styles using a `typography` preset with no `textShadow` at all (the sky-data grid, `CardLabel`, the periodic-assessment-due banner, all of Today's Check-In's own internal text) and added it to each. Found the border width actually had two tiers already, `TAB_BORDER_WIDTH` (2px) for boxes whose border color is dynamically set to a tab identity color, and a plain 1px for smaller controls (buttons, pills, chips, the text input), which were each already internally consistent. The actual inconsistency was 3 cards (`loadingCard`, `allSectionsHiddenCard`, `greetingCard`) left at the old 1px specifically because their border carries no tab-color meaning, a reasoned distinction at the time it was made, but one that read as visual inconsistency once actually seen next to every other card on the same screen. Moved all 3 to `TAB_BORDER_WIDTH`, and `moreFlipCard`'s own already-matching hardcoded `2` now references the same shared constant. `TAB_BORDER_WIDTH`'s own comment updated in place to explain the current, correct reasoning rather than leave the superseded one standing.

`tsc` clean project-wide; `eslint` clean on every touched file (2 new unescaped-apostrophe issues introduced by this pass's own new Profile text were caught and fixed; several more pre-existing ones elsewhere in `profile.tsx`/`index.tsx`, unrelated to this change, confirmed via `git diff` and left untouched). Five files touched (`app/(tabs)/index.tsx`, `app/profile.tsx`, `lib/db.ts` new table plus 2 new functions). **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Most recent (2026-08-23): a disk-space audit, direct request, "scan the entire build of the app looking for unnecessary code or notes or anything that adds to bloating the app."** Measured rather than guessed: `du` across every top-level folder, `git ls-files` to separate what's actually tracked from local-only build output, `git count-objects`/`.git/lfs` inspection for repository history size, and a cross-reference of every image in `assets/` against every `require()` in the source tree.

**Fixed outright, zero risk, git-tracked and confirmed dead:** 17 files, roughly 8.4MB. Nine `.bak`/`.bak2`/`.bak3` snapshots left over from the icon-redesign work (`icon.png`, `android-icon-foreground.png`, `android-icon-monochrome.png`, each backed up 3 times, none ever read by any config or `require()`), 2 more (`seed-transparent.png.bak`/`.bak2`) backing up a file that was already deleted outright back on 2026-08-21, 2 confirmed-unreferenced background images (`App_Background_Image.png`, `Fruits_Vegetables.png`, both explicitly superseded and left in place at the time per this document's own 2026-08-21/22 entries), and 4 unused default Expo template assets (`react-logo.png` and its `@2x`/`@3x` variants, `partial-react-logo.png`, confirmed against every `.tsx`/`.ts`/`.json` file in the project, `splash-icon.png` is the one template image actually still wired into `app.json` and was left alone). Every one of these already has its full history preserved in git regardless, so nothing is actually lost.

**Named, not touched, because each one needs a deliberate decision rather than a safe default:**
- `.git`'s own local size is 2.0GB, and 1.8GB of that is the local git-lfs object cache holding 13 historical versions of the two tracked databases (`foods_reference.db`, `unified_foods.sqlite`). `git lfs prune` would reclaim most of that safely (it only drops local cache copies still recoverable from the LFS remote), but it's an operation on repository state, not something to run unprompted.
- `android/` is 2.1GB of local native build output, entirely untracked by git, regenerates automatically on the next native build. Safe to delete for disk space, at the cost of a rebuild next time one is needed.
- `ClaudeWork/` is 267MB of raw source-data downloads and early build scripts from the original database-building work in mid-July (national nutrition datasets, an old `build_food_database.py` v1/v2/v3, even a duplicate copy of one .zip download), untracked, and not read by the actual production pipeline (`scripts/build_food_reference_db.py` reads from a different OneDrive folder entirely). Looks disposable, not confirmed disposable, since some of these downloads could be hard to re-fetch if ever needed again.
- `unified-database/` (254MB) and `node_modules/` (3.8GB) are both expected, both explained elsewhere in this document, neither is bloat to fix.

**On the comments themselves**, since "unnecessary... notes" was named directly: this codebase's own header-comment convention (the detailed build-journal style visible throughout `lib/`, `scripts/`, and this document) does not bloat the shipped app. Metro's production bundler strips comments during minification, so none of this reaches an installed phone; the only cost is source-repository size, and plain text compresses efficiently in git, a small cost next to the LFS history above. Not changed, and not treated as the same category of problem the asset backups were.

`tsc` clean project-wide after the deletions (nothing in code referenced any of the 17 removed files, confirming the audit's own cross-reference was accurate before anything was deleted). **Confirmable on-device only in the negative: nothing should look different, since none of the removed files were ever shown to begin with.**

**Most recent (2026-08-23): five more direct reports on the Digest, delivered together in one message.** The search subheader's shadow needed to reach the true screen edges, not just its own padded content box; the Glossary needed far more coverage than it had; Glossary itself needed pulling out of the alphabetized topic list into its own dedicated button; the Information icon needed to move into the search field itself; and the Digest's own identity color needed to read as purple, not pink.

(1) The shadow: `EdgeShadow` fills whatever box it sits in as a plain flex child, and `fixedHeader`'s own horizontal padding kept it short of the true edges on every screen. A new `FIXED_HEADER_HORIZONTAL_PADDING` constant plus an `edgeShadowFullWidth` style (a matching negative `marginHorizontal`) cancels that padding for the shadow specifically, without touching the header's own padded content.

(2) Glossary content: 14 new, individually-verified terms added (DASH Diet, SCOBY, NOVA Classification, CAR-T Cell Therapy, PNPLA3, CGM, TRAb & TSI, DDT, DAS28 & CDAI, Confidence Interval, CBC, HIF & VEGF, JC Virus, MRI & CT), each confirmed by actual usage across the Digest content base, each `relatedIds` entry checked to resolve to an existing entry before shipping (two guessed IDs caught and corrected mid-build). 100 to 114 total, named honestly as a continuation of the same ongoing depth work rather than a claim of completeness.

(3) The Glossary button: pulled out of the alphabetized Basic Health topic menu, since it never belonged sorted in among "Diet & Food" and "Hormones," and given its own button above the search bar, to the right of the breadcrumb link. Building it surfaced a bug before it shipped: the first draft assumed every glossary entry lives under Basic Health's own category, which would have shown only 55 of the 114 terms, since 45 span 13 other condition categories. Rebuilt around a new `glossaryOpen` state and a cross-category `allGlossaryEntries` list, filtered straight from the full, unfiltered entry pool, reusing the same `BasicHealthShelves` shelf renderer every other topic drill-in already uses rather than a new view.

(4) The Information icon: moved from its own separate button above the search bar into the search field's own right edge (a new `onPressInfo` prop on `DigestSearchInput`), on every category's search bar at once since they all share one implementation; the mic keeps its existing left-side position via `NAVIGATION_HAND`. Worth naming as a known gap: if `NAVIGATION_HAND` is ever actually flipped to right-handed, the mic and this new info icon would collide, since the info icon's own side is fixed rather than handedness-aware.

(5) The color: `colors.tabPurpleDigest` moved from `#C7ACF6` to `#AF88F2`, same hue (262°), lightness and saturation both pulled down (82%/80% to 74%/72%), since the "way more pink than purple" read came from how light and saturated the color was, not its measured hue. Checked by contrast math, not by eye alone: still 5.27:1 against `colors.textOnPrimary` on filled buttons, comfortably past the 4.5:1 floor, but the same darkening drops the color's own use as label text directly on `colors.surface`/`colors.menuSurface` to roughly 2.5-2.7:1, under this file's documented 3:1 floor for that context. Accepted as a trade-off rather than re-lightened back, since the affected labels (TabHub/LensHub picker text, `categoryHeaderText`) already carry `menuLabelShadow` as mitigation and the filled-button use is the more load-bearing one. Worth an on-device look, not just this math, before calling it settled.

Caught in passing while researching this batch, unrelated to any of the five requests: three more instances of the truncated "What's:"/"This is:" sentence bug (`type1Diabetes.ts`, `multipleSclerosis.ts`, `psoriasis.ts`), the same class of bug a prior session believed fully swept in `graves.ts` with none left elsewhere. All three fixed, and a fresh, broader sweep confirmed zero remaining instances this time. `tsc`/`eslint` clean across every touched file (`purple-digest.tsx`, `constants/colors.ts`, `lib/digest/glossary.ts`, plus the three incidental content files). **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Same-day follow-up, direct correction to part (5) above, on-device:** "you darkened the font on the Digest purple headings and topics. Those need to go back to the lighter shade of purple." This confirms rather than contradicts the tradeoff already named in the color's own comment: `tabPurpleDigest` reading as text directly on `colors.surface`/`menuSurface` (category headers, topic menu labels, shelf and entry titles) was exactly the marginal-contrast gap flagged as needing an on-device look, and the look came back negative. Rather than re-lighten the shared token itself, which would undo the fix every button, border, and icon use still needed, a new `colors.tabPurpleDigestText` restores the pre-darkening `#C7ACF6` as its own token, used only where this color paints text. All 15 text-color uses in `purple-digest.tsx` (`categoryHeaderText`, `shelfHeading`, `digestTopicMenuItemLabel`, `shelfCardTitle`, `cardTitle`, `demoHeading`/`demoSubheading`, `detailLabel`, `citationsLabel`, `relatedLabel`, `recipeNutritionLabel`, `searchResultCategory`, `matchTermPillText`, `crossConditionPillText`, `dynamicActionButtonText`) switched to it; every fill, border, and icon use stays on the darker value. Same split TabHub/LensHub's own label color already draws via `lighten`/`MENU_LABEL_LIGHTEN_FRACTION`: the underlying identity color and the color actually painted as on-screen font are allowed to differ on purpose. `tsc`/`eslint` clean, two files touched. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Same-day follow-up, direct request: "go back through and add the darker drop shadow back to all of the purple fonts."** `menuLabelShadow` (the stronger of the two shared text-shadow constants, already used on 3 of the 15 purple text styles above) is now applied to the remaining 12: `shelfHeading`, `digestTopicMenuItemLabel`, `searchResultCategory`, `matchTermPillText`, `demoHeading`/`demoSubheading`, `crossConditionPillText`, `detailLabel`, `dynamicActionButtonText`, `recipeNutritionLabel`, `citationsLabel`, `relatedLabel`. Now every text style using the Digest's lighter purple carries the same shadow, matching the treatment already standing on `categoryHeaderText`/`shelfCardTitle`/`cardTitle`, so the lighter shade stays legible against the photo background rather than reintroducing the class of contrast bug this same shadow was already fixed for elsewhere in the app. `tsc`/`eslint` clean, one file touched. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Also 2026-08-23: Basic Health's "More" catch-all audited, and its 8 stranded entries given a home.** Direct question: "In Basic Health there are 8 entries in the More section. It says they don't have another group to be contained in. That might have been true before but how about now? Do they fit somewhere now?" Checked directly against the live entry list rather than assumed: all 8 (`bodyfat-heritability-twin-study`, `bodyfat-constrained-energy-expenditure`, `bodyfat-hadza-cardiovascular-health`, `bodyfat-visceral-vs-subcutaneous`, `lipodystrophy-fat-necessity`, `adiponectin-overview`, `pbn-ornish-lifestyle-heart-trial`, `pbn-esselstyn-cohort-longterm`) trace to the NOVA fat-biology and Ornish/Esselstyn plant-based research batches built 2026-08-21/23, added after `BASIC_HEALTH_TOPICS`' own prefix list was last set and never given a prefix of their own. `lipodystrophy-`/`adiponectin-` joined the existing `Hormones` subtopic (the same fat-hormone research cluster every `leptin-` entry there already belongs to); the 4 `bodyfat-` entries got a new `Body Fat Biology` subtopic alongside it (population-level body-fat biology, not one specific hormone); the 2 `pbn-` entries joined `Popular Diets & Eating Styles` (trial evidence for one dietary philosophy, the same shape every other entry there already covers). Verified by extraction script against the content files themselves: 0 of 501 Basic Health entries now fall through to "More." `tsc`/`eslint` clean, one file touched.

**Also 2026-08-23: a new Earth Matters topic, Climate Science & the Weather Machine, built from a 2019 NOVA documentary the user is currently watching.** Direct request: "I am now watching a documentary from 2019 called Decoding the weather machine. This data is part of the Inside Story of our planet and belongs in the Earth Matters area. Research everything about the topics discussed in this documentation, branch from it to determine what is up to date now about all of that data and create a place, if it doesn't already exist, where this data deserves to be." No place for climate science existed in Earth Matters before this: its 10 existing topics are entirely about the organic/regenerative-agriculture movement.

New `lib/digest/climateScience.ts`, 10 entries, none sourced from the documentary's own footage or narration: every entry traces to the agency page, satellite record, or peer-reviewed study the film's own segment is actually built on, individually verified via WebSearch, each explicitly checked for what has changed since the film's 2019 vantage point. The greenhouse effect mechanism and the Mauna Loa CO2 record (NASA, NOAA, Scripps) anchor the batch; the ice-core paleoclimate record the film describes as reaching roughly 800,000 years is updated to the January 2025 Beyond EPICA announcement reaching 1.2 million; climate model reliability cites the Hausfather 2020 verification study plus NASA GISS's own confirmation; extreme weather attribution science, still young in 2019, is shown matured into a repeatable, partly peer-reviewed World Weather Attribution methodology; global temperature status cites the WMO's own 2025 State of the Global Climate report (2015-2025 the eleven warmest years on record). Three entries branch beyond what the film covers at all: satellite-measured sea level rise acceleration and GRACE/GRACE-FO polar ice sheet mass loss (the direct physical consequence side of the film's own hurricane/drought segments), the Armstrong McKay 2022 Science synthesis on climate tipping-point risk (a term the film uses without quantifying), and renewable energy's own cost and deployment growth since 2019 via the IEA's 2025 renewables report, the mitigation side of the story the film's own closing segment has no numbers for.

Every entry carries an honestly tiered evidence level, not a blanket "strong": the ice-core age is confirmed but its full climate record is still under analysis (tiered `moderate`), individual rapid attribution studies are released before full peer review even though the underlying method is peer-reviewed (`moderate`), tipping-point thresholds are named consequences of well-established mechanisms but their specific timing carries acknowledged uncertainty (`moderate`), and the renewable-energy forecast is itself a moving target, the IEA's own 2025 figure is a downward revision from its 2024 one (`moderate`). Wired into `purple-digest.tsx` as a new `EarthMattersTopic` ("Climate Science & the Weather Machine," placed right after the soil-science lead as the category's other planetary-systems foundation) with its own `id.startsWith('climate-')` classification rule, and into `lib/digest/index.ts`'s entry list. A first pass, named honestly, not exhaustive: this is a two-hour documentary built around dozens of named scientists and field locations, and this batch covers its core scientific throughlines with current, individually verified sourcing rather than reconstructing every scene. `tsc`/`eslint` clean, three files touched (`lib/digest/climateScience.ts` new, `lib/digest/index.ts`, `purple-digest.tsx`). **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Same-day follow-up: a "why" explanation for interaction warnings, scoped deliberately after a direct architecture conversation.** Asked how Digest content stays current once the app is "done," then floated a live in-app AI, both to keep content fresh (a Windows-Update-style background refresh) and to answer "why shouldn't I take X with Y" questions. Laid out the tradeoffs rather than building either unprompted: a live AI call answering a GENERIC question needs no personal data and is architecturally simple; a live call reasoning over what someone actually logged means sending local health data off-device, a direct conflict with this app's own stated "no backend storing anyone's health data" stance, not a decision to make by default. Asked which to build first: **"Let's start with generic explanations, no personal data involved."** Then asked a second, narrower question, since "generic, no personal data" still leaves open whether the explanation is pre-written or generated live: pre-written won outright, ships now, costs nothing per use, works offline, never sends anything off the device.

Built as `mechanism`, a new nullable column on the live `interaction_rules` table (`assets/data/foods_reference.db`, patched via a new `scripts/add_interaction_rule_mechanisms.py`, the same supplemental-patch pattern `add_interaction_rules.py`/`add_my_meds_reference_data.py` already established), threaded through `InteractionRuleRecord` (`lib/db.ts`) and both `InteractionWarning`/`ReferenceOnlyRule` (`lib/interactionRules.ts`, all 8 construction sites in `evaluateInteractionRules`). A new shared `components/WhyExplainer.tsx` renders a small tappable "Why?" wherever a rule has one, reusing each screen's own already-instantiated `showInfoAlert` rather than mounting a second modal, wired into all 6 places interaction rules render (Insights' My Meds view, and 4 separate lenses on Schedule) instead of duplicating the same small block six times.

Content: 13 of the table's 44 rows now have an individually-verified mechanism explanation, covering the classic food/supplement/prescription timing and absorption cluster (calcium/iron, calcium/zinc, the 4 fat-soluble-vitamin rules, levothyroxine's own 3 timing rules with calcium/iron/magnesium, biotin's thyroid-lab interference, metformin's TSH-lowering effect, vitamin K2/warfarin, aspirin/ibuprofen), the rules a person is most likely to actually ask "why" about. Two (the levothyroxine/calcium chelation mechanism and biotin's own streptavidin-assay interference) were independently verified via WebSearch since the row's existing citation named the regulatory finding but not the underlying biology; the rest trace to the same citation the row already carried. The remaining 31 rows (mostly the age-threshold and concurrent-use cautions added across later condition build-outs) are a named, scoped-out follow-up, not forgotten. `REFERENCE_DB_VERSION` bumped, so this forces the same re-import on next launch every reference-database patch already does. `tsc`/`eslint` clean across every touched file. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Same-day follow-up: the remaining 31 mechanisms, in the same request as the disk-space audit above.** New `scripts/add_interaction_rule_mechanisms_batch2.py`, same pattern as the first script, reading its own 31 paragraphs from a companion plain-text file (`scripts/interaction_rule_mechanisms_batch2.txt`) rather than hand-escaping that much prose into Python string literals. Covers the age-threshold Beers Criteria cautions (diphenhydramine, glyburide, lithium, NSAIDs, long-term PPI use), the grapefruit/CYP3A4 cluster (atorvastatin, cyclosporine, hydroxychloroquine, three separate rows sharing one underlying mechanism, described distinctly enough in each row's own text not to read as copy-pasted), the xanthine-oxidase pair (`allopurinol_azathioprine_toxicity`/`azathioprine_allopurinol_toxicity`, two existing rows describing the identical interaction from each drug's own side, not a duplicate this pass introduced), the TNF-inhibitor infection-risk pair, thyroid-drug-specific rows (antithyroid/iodine, methimazole's own agranulocytosis, PTU's own liver injury, radioactive iodine prep), and the remaining standalone rows (ACE/ARB potassium monitoring, acitretin/alcohol, colchicine/statin myopathy, dimethyl fumarate flushing, insulin/alcohol delayed hypoglycemia, methotrexate's own three rows, natalizumab/PML, spironolactone/potassium, sulfonylurea/kidney function, sumatriptan/SSRI serotonin syndrome, pediatric aspirin/Reye's, pediatric DKA/cerebral edema). Three mechanisms (Reye's syndrome's own mitochondrial injury pathway, pediatric DKA cerebral edema's own still-unsettled mechanism, methimazole agranulocytosis' immune-mediated basis) were independently verified via WebSearch rather than assumed, the cerebral-edema entry states directly that the older osmotic-swelling explanation has not held up against newer evidence pointing toward reduced brain blood flow instead, an unresolved scientific question rather than a settled one dressed up as certain. All 44 of the table's rows now carry a mechanism; nothing is left in the earlier "scoped, not-yet-done" state. `REFERENCE_DB_VERSION` bumped again. `tsc` clean project-wide (no source files touched this half, only the database and the two new script/data files). **Not yet confirmed on-device.**

**Same-day follow-up, direct request: rewrite Earth Matters' own closing synthesis card to cover the whole category, not just one sub-cluster.** "Update the Putting it all together in Earth Matters so that it takes in account everything that is there now based on the information contained in them all together. Label it 'What Does This All Mean?' and then provide a well laid out analysis of it all, broken into paragraphs and well written with[out] any AI tics happening. Project a little but only based on the evidence." The prior version of this entry (`foodhistory-regen-tying-together`) only synthesized one sub-cluster, the regenerative-agriculture regional-adoption comparison, not the category's other 10 topics including the same day's own new climate-science addition. Rewritten in place (same id, so every existing cross-link into it still resolves): title now "What Does This All Mean?", the summary restructured into seven distinct paragraphs (the first multi-paragraph entry in this Digest, using literal line breaks the shared `Text` component already renders correctly) tracing the throughline connecting soil, atmosphere, pollinators, and the human gut; naming what across the category is directly measured versus still actively disputed; naming the documented reversal case studies (Niger, Brazil, China) as proof decline is not inevitable; naming the lobbying and policy-structure gap actually holding restoration back; naming the specific, evidenced individual levers (boycotts, institutional purchasing, shareholder resolutions) the category itself documents; and a closing paragraph projecting only as far as the assembled evidence supports, that reversal is demonstrated but its pace against current economic and political structures is the one question this category cannot yet settle. `relatedIds` expanded from 8 to 19, now touching every one of the category's 11 topics at least once, including two of the new Climate Science entries. `citations` set to `[]` rather than reused, since the prior citations backed specific regional-market figures no longer restated in the new text, matching the precedent `foodhistory-opinion-synthesis` already sets for a synthesis-of-other-entries type of card. Checked line by line for the writing-style rule this request itself named: zero instances of "real"/"genuine(ly)"/"honest(ly)"/"worth" as filler, zero dashes-as-punctuation, zero "it's not X, it's Y" constructions. `tsc`/`eslint` clean, one file touched. **Not yet confirmed on-device.**
